- Team Creativity, Cognition, and Cognitive Style Diversity
12. Q: How should a team evaluate advice from an outsider whose competence is uncertain?
Show AnswerA: Evaluate the proposed solution independently of the contributor's prestige, confidence, or familiarity with the organization. First, clarify the actual problem the advice is intended to solve. Next, identify the assumptions, evidence, and logic behind the suggestion. Determine whether the proposal addresses the root cause or only an apparent symptom. Then test the parts that can be evaluated through a small experiment, simulation, prototype, independent calculation, or comparison with known results. In a high-risk field, including medicine, cybersecurity, finance, or legal compliance, the assessment must also consider professional requirements and the consequences of being wrong. Outsiders may offer useful reframing, but they may not understand every constraint that insiders must satisfy. Invite them to reveal overlooked possibilities, then ask qualified people to assess the proposal before implementation. This process preserves the benefits of diverse input without confusing novelty with reliability or allowing an unconventional suggestion to bypass essential controls.
Study instruction:
- Create a review checklist that separates the source of an idea from its evidence, feasibility, risks, and measurable outcomes.
Key point:
- Outsiders can expand the range of possible solutions.
- Evidence and domain-specific safeguards determine whether a solution should be adopted.
Source links:
- How Does Diversity Affect Team Cognitive Processes? — Academy of Management Annals
13. Q: How can teams distinguish essential quality standards from unnecessary perfectionism?
Show AnswerA: Teams often mistake maximum technical refinement for the most effective solution. In practice, the appropriate quality level depends on the purpose of the work, its users, the cost of errors, and the resources available. Some components require exactness because an error could threaten safety, legal compliance, financial integrity, or trust. Other components may need only to function reliably enough to support the intended result. The chapter's account of Brad Bird illustrates this distinction through animation production: not every image must be developed through the most elaborate technical process if a simpler method achieves the desired audience experience. The broader lesson is to define acceptance criteria before investing in refinement. Teams should establish minimum requirements, identify high-risk components that demand stronger assurance, and test whether further improvements produce meaningful benefits. This reduces waste while preserving quality where it matters most. It also makes it easier to determine whether delays reflect genuine technical limitations or standards that the team has never critically examined.
Study instruction:
- Choose a delayed project and classify its requirements into mandatory quality conditions, useful enhancements, and features that could be simplified or deferred.
Key point:
- Quality should be defined in relation to purpose, risk, and user needs.
- Efficient delivery requires distinguishing essential precision from avoidable refinement.
Source links:
- Team Creativity, Cognition, and Cognitive Style Diversity
14. Q: What is the difference between demographic diversity, professional diversity, and cognitive diversity?
Show AnswerA: Demographic diversity concerns differences in characteristics such as gender, age, and other social or personal attributes. Professional diversity reflects differences in training, occupation, disciplinary knowledge, experience, and industry background. Cognitive diversity concerns differences in how people interpret information, structure problems, evaluate evidence, and generate solutions. These categories can overlap, but none automatically guarantees the others. Two people with different professional titles may use nearly identical reasoning, while two colleagues in the same profession may approach a problem in very different ways. Demographic diversity can broaden experience and challenge assumptions, but its practical impact depends on the knowledge, opportunities, and perspectives people contribute. Organizations should therefore consider both representation and participation. A diverse roster may provide little benefit if everyone is expected to conform to one dominant viewpoint. Conversely, a group can include several cognitive approaches while remaining demographically unrepresentative. Effective team design considers the task, the expertise needed, the experiences missing from current discussions, and whether all members can contribute meaningfully.
Study instruction:
- For a current team, separately assess demographic representation, functional expertise, and diversity of problem-solving approaches rather than treating them as interchangeable measures.
Key point:
- Diversity has several dimensions that should be distinguished and evaluated separately.
- Representation creates potential value, while inclusive participation helps translate it into useful contributions.
Source links:
- How Does Diversity Affect Team Cognitive Processes? — Academy of Management Annals
15. Q: What does research on gender-diverse scientific teams suggest about innovation?
Show AnswerA: The chapter discusses research examining millions of scientific publications and the relationship between the gender composition of research teams and the novelty and impact of their work. A related study published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences analyzed 6.6 million medical-science papers and reported that mixed-gender teams produced more novel and more highly cited research than comparable single-gender teams in its analyses. The findings support the proposition that teams with different experiences and perspectives can generate distinctive ideas. They should not be interpreted as proof that gender composition alone guarantees stronger performance or that all teams benefit equally in every circumstance. Publication data are observational and remain subject to the limitations of how teams, outputs, and impact are measured. Practical implementation also matters: members must be able to express different viewpoints and participate in decisions. Organizations should use diversity evidence to guide fair recruitment, participation, collaboration, and evaluation rather than treating demographic composition as an automatic substitute for team effectiveness.
Study instruction:
- Read the study abstract and distinguish its reported statistical associations from stronger claims about causation or individual team outcomes.
Key point:
- Research associates gender diversity with greater novelty and impact in scientific publications.
- Diversity is a valuable design consideration, not a guarantee of success or a replacement for sound collaboration.

16. Q: How can a historically homogeneous research team overlook important questions?
Show AnswerA: Research teams tend to investigate questions that seem significant from the perspectives and experiences of their members. If a team draws from a narrow range of backgrounds, certain experiences may receive less attention or remain absent from research design. The chapter describes historical examples in medical research where studies disproportionately enrolled men, potentially limiting the ability to generalize their findings to women. It also explains how increasing women's participation in medical research helped bring greater attention to health issues that earlier teams had underemphasized. The broader principle is that the assumptions used to define a research question can determine which problems become visible. Different participants may recognize overlooked populations, missing variables, unsuitable measures, or limitations in existing interpretations. This does not mean that identity alone determines a person's research interests or that demographic similarity invalidates a study. Rather, researchers should examine representation, sampling, relevance, and the range of perspectives involved in setting priorities. Deliberately reviewing whose experiences are absent can improve the quality and applicability of research.
Study instruction:
- Examine a research project and identify which populations, circumstances, or outcomes were excluded from the design and how those exclusions affect interpretation.
Key point:
- Narrow representation can contribute to blind spots in research questions and evidence.
- Inclusive research design requires examining who is represented and whose experiences are missing.

17. Q: What is an intellectual echo chamber, and how can it obstruct progress?
Show AnswerA: An intellectual echo chamber is an environment in which ideas repeatedly circulate among people who share similar assumptions, methods, and sources of information. Members may continue producing work, but their efforts can become increasingly incremental because they investigate variations of questions that the group already considers important. Similarity can make communication efficient, yet it can also reduce the chance that an alternative interpretation will be introduced. Agreement may then be mistaken for independent confirmation even when the apparent consensus comes from a narrow, interconnected network. To detect this problem, examine whether the same people repeatedly review one another's ideas, whether external criticism influences decisions, and whether the team regularly encounters approaches from other disciplines. A practical response is to invite independent review, compare explanations from unrelated fields, and identify assumptions that have not been tested. The aim is not to eliminate specialization or agreement, but to ensure that conclusions survive examination beyond a limited circle of familiar thinkers.
Study instruction:
- Map the information sources behind a recent decision and identify whether apparently independent opinions relied on the same underlying evidence.
Key point:
- Repeated agreement among connected people may conceal shared blind spots.
- Independent perspectives help test whether established conclusions remain convincing outside the existing network.
Source links:
- Networks, Creativity, and Time: Staying Creative through Brokerage and Network Rejuvenation
18. Q: Under what conditions is diversity especially valuable for problem-solving?
Show AnswerA: Diversity is particularly useful when a task is complex, unfamiliar, ambiguous, or resistant to established methods. Such problems usually contain several interacting factors, making it difficult for one person or homogeneous team to identify every relevant consideration. Different contributors may recognize different risks, propose alternative explanations, or connect knowledge from otherwise separate fields. Diversity can also be valuable when a problem requires innovation rather than reliable repetition of a well-understood procedure. By contrast, a brief, routine task with a known correct method may benefit more from standardization and efficient coordination than from extensive debate. This distinction does not make diversity irrelevant to routine work; it means that its benefits should be matched to the task. Managers should ask whether the current challenge requires new information or a new interpretation. When it does, a targeted set of different perspectives may be more valuable than adding further people with the same expertise. The complexity and novelty of the task should guide the design of the problem-solving process.
Study instruction:
- Classify three tasks as routine or complex, then justify whether each needs standardized execution, diverse consultation, or both.
Key point:
- Diverse perspectives are especially useful when familiar methods cannot adequately resolve a problem.
- The team composition and decision process should fit the complexity of the task.
Source links:
- How Does Diversity Affect Team Cognitive Processes? — Academy of Management Annals
19. Q: How can diversity make teamwork more difficult, and what should managers do about it?
Show AnswerA: Different perspectives do not automatically combine into a coherent solution. People may use different terminology, disagree about what counts as evidence, prioritize competing objectives, or misinterpret unfamiliar approaches. Members may also form subgroups around shared identities or professional backgrounds, limiting information exchange across those boundaries. If a team lacks a clear objective or cannot manage disagreement, diversity can create friction without producing better decisions. Managers should establish common goals, define the decision to be made, make responsibilities explicit, and create structured opportunities for different viewpoints to be evaluated. Shared terminology and summaries of competing proposals help participants understand disagreements accurately. It is also useful to distinguish disagreements about facts from differences in priorities or risk tolerance. The appropriate goal is not universal consensus; it is an informed decision that considers relevant alternatives and makes trade-offs explicit. Teams can benefit from diversity when they develop the communication and coordination practices required to use it productively.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a meeting procedure that allows each participant to present evidence, state assumptions, challenge proposals respectfully, and record unresolved disagreements.
Key point:
- Diversity creates opportunities as well as coordination challenges.
- Clear goals and structured discussion help convert differences into useful analysis.
Source links:
- How Does Diversity Affect Team Cognitive Processes? — Academy of Management Annals
20. Q: What does Shane Battier's basketball career illustrate about unconventional contributions to team performance?
Show AnswerA: The chapter presents Shane Battier as an example of a contributor whose value was not fully captured by traditional individual statistics. His strengths included analyzing opponents, using detailed information to select defensive strategies, and making decisions that benefited team performance even when they did not improve his own scoring numbers. His contribution was partly complementary: he helped teammates perform their roles more effectively while limiting the opposition's strengths. The broader lesson is that the value of a team member depends on how that person changes the performance of the entire system, not merely on visible individual output. An unusual combination of analytical ability, strategic judgment, and selflessness may be especially useful when it addresses a team's neglected needs. However, the example should not be treated as proof that individual statistics are generally useless. The appropriate lesson is to identify the outcome that matters, determine which contributions influence it, and avoid relying on measures that capture only the most visible parts of performance.
Study instruction:
- Identify a role in your workplace whose contribution may be overlooked because the performance indicators focus on visible individual output rather than team results.
Key point:
- Complementary contributions can improve collective performance without producing impressive individual metrics.
- Performance assessment should reflect the actual objective and the interactions among team members.

21. Q: How can organizations recognize contributions that traditional performance metrics fail to capture?
Show AnswerA: Traditional indicators often measure individual output, such as the number of tasks completed, sales generated, or errors detected. These figures are useful, but they may omit contributions that make colleagues more effective, prevent problems, improve coordination, or create opportunities for others. In basketball, plus-minus statistics attempt to capture how the team's score changes while a player is on the court. The measure is imperfect because team composition, opponents, and playing conditions also influence the result, but it illustrates a broader principle: assess performance in context. In organizations, complementary measures might include reduced rework, better handoffs, improved decision quality, fewer unresolved risks, or contributions to shared knowledge. Managers should combine quantitative indicators with evidence about an individual's role, responsibilities, and working environment. No single metric should determine conclusions where outcomes depend on multiple interacting factors. A more complete evaluation considers both direct output and how effectively the person's work improves the performance of the wider system.
Study instruction:
- Review a performance scorecard and identify at least one valuable contribution it measures poorly, then propose evidence that could supplement the existing metrics.
Key point:
- Individual output and contribution to collective outcomes are not identical.
- Multiple complementary measures provide a more complete view of performance.
Source links:
- Team Creativity, Cognition, and Cognitive Style Diversity
22. Q: How does crowdsourcing help people solve problems that have resisted conventional approaches?
Show AnswerA: Crowdsourcing invites a wider group of contributors to provide information, ideas, analyses, or solutions to a defined problem. It is especially promising when a problem has remained unresolved despite repeated attempts by a limited group of specialists. Different contributors may recognize patterns, ask unfamiliar questions, or connect information that the original problem-solvers have not combined. The process can also distribute effort across participants with different capabilities. However, a large number of responses does not guarantee a reliable answer. Some contributions may be inaccurate, repetitive, irrelevant, or unsupported by evidence. Effective crowdsourcing therefore requires a well-defined question, useful context, appropriate contributors, a method for organizing submissions, and a process for validating promising answers. In high-stakes fields, crowd suggestions should complement rather than replace qualified professional judgment. The key advantage is the breadth of possible approaches; the key responsibility is to identify which contributions are sufficiently credible and useful to warrant further testing.
Study instruction:
- Define a problem suitable for external input and specify what information contributors need, how responses will be compared, and who will validate promising suggestions.
Key point:
- Crowdsourcing expands the range of potential solutions.
- A structured review process is necessary to distinguish useful contributions from noise.

23. Q: What does the CrowdMed example show about using diverse perspectives in difficult medical cases?
Show AnswerA: The chapter describes CrowdMed as an online platform where people with unresolved medical cases could receive diagnostic suggestions from a distributed community of contributors. The approach brought together people with different backgrounds, including individuals outside mainstream medical practice, in an effort to identify possibilities that earlier consultations might have missed. An independent evaluation of CrowdMed examined 397 completed cases from 2013 to 2015. It found that 59.6% of surveyed patients who answered the relevant question reported that the process provided insights bringing them closer to a correct diagnosis. This finding indicates perceived usefulness, not proof that the platform reliably established correct diagnoses in every case. The evaluation itself called for further validation and long-term assessment. The general lesson is that diverse contributors can generate leads when conventional routes have stalled, but those leads require clinical assessment and appropriate follow-up. Crowdsourced suggestions must not be treated as verified diagnoses or as a substitute for qualified medical care.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish a reported improvement in diagnostic insight from a clinically confirmed diagnosis, and identify the evidence needed to establish each outcome.
Key point:
- Diverse contributions can help generate new diagnostic possibilities.
- Medical suggestions require professional validation and appropriate clinical oversight.

24. Q: How can an open innovation challenge improve a practical product or design?
Show AnswerA: An open innovation challenge allows people with different skills to contribute to a design problem that may fall outside the experience of its original owner. The chapter's account of a pill bottle for a person with Parkinson's disease illustrates this process. A problem with accessing medication attracted interest from designers, engineers, and people familiar with 3D printing. A contributor without extensive product-design experience proposed a concept, and others helped develop and refine the prototype. The example shows how an idea can improve through successive contributions, even when the original proposer lacks every skill required to complete it. Practical innovation can emerge from a sequence of partial solutions rather than one person possessing the entire answer. To reproduce this approach, define the user problem clearly, share relevant constraints, invite complementary contributions, test prototypes with intended users, and record revisions. Safety, accessibility, usability, cost, and manufacturability should be assessed before a design is adopted or distributed.
Study instruction:
- Take a common usability problem and design a small prototype challenge that involves people from at least two different areas of expertise and feedback from intended users.
Key point:
- Complex products can improve through contributions from people with complementary skills.
- Repeated testing and refinement turn initial ideas into more usable solutions.
Source links:
- How Does Diversity Affect Team Cognitive Processes? — Academy of Management Annals
25. Q: When should a person consult an expert, an unfamiliar outsider, a crowd, or an internal second perspective?
Show AnswerA: The appropriate source of advice depends on the problem's characteristics. An expert is particularly useful when a task requires specialized knowledge, established technical methods, or compliance with professional standards. An unfamiliar outsider may be more helpful when the problem has become trapped in established assumptions and a new framing is needed. A crowd is potentially valuable when a problem can be described clearly, many people can contribute meaningfully, and the resulting suggestions can be evaluated systematically. An internal second perspective is practical when external assistance is unavailable, although it cannot fully reproduce another person's experience. These options are complementary rather than mutually exclusive. For example, an organization might use outsiders to generate alternative approaches, a broader group to surface possibilities, and experts to validate the strongest candidates. The choice should also reflect confidentiality, cost, urgency, and risk. A high-stakes problem requires more rigorous validation than a low-cost exploratory experiment. The objective is to obtain the type of perspective the problem actually lacks.
Study instruction:
- Compare four potential advice sources for one unresolved problem and justify the choice based on expertise, novelty, cost, confidentiality, and validation requirements.
Key point:
- Different advice sources solve different information and perspective gaps.
- Use novel input for exploration and qualified evidence for validation where necessary.
Source links:
- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
26. Q: How should an organization design a crowdsourcing process from problem definition to implementation?
Show AnswerA: Start by defining the problem, the desired outcome, and the constraints that any solution must satisfy. Provide contributors with enough background information to understand the challenge without revealing confidential or unnecessary personal data. Identify the types of participants who could supply distinct knowledge, and choose a channel appropriate to the task. Establish how contributions will be submitted, documented, reviewed, and tested. Where feasible, ask participants to explain their reasoning and identify assumptions rather than submitting conclusions alone. Group similar suggestions, distinguish genuinely different approaches, and select candidates for further evaluation using predefined criteria. Assign a responsible owner to coordinate testing and ensure that the process does not end with a collection of unimplemented ideas. Record which proposals were accepted, rejected, or deferred and explain the reasons. Finally, measure the outcome against the original objective and capture lessons for future challenges. Crowdsourcing works best as a managed problem-solving process, not as an unstructured request for opinions.
Study instruction:
- Draft a one-page crowdsourcing plan specifying the problem, contributor groups, submission format, evaluation criteria, safeguards, decision owner, and success measures.
Key point:
- Clearly defined problems produce more useful contributions.
- Evaluation, ownership, and implementation are essential parts of the process.
Source links:
- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
27. Q: What is the wisdom of the internal crowd, and why is it useful when external advice is unavailable?
Show AnswerA: The wisdom of the internal crowd refers to methods through which one person generates more than one sufficiently independent perspective on a question. A person can revisit an estimate, consider an alternative explanation, or deliberately argue against an initial conclusion. The resulting answers can then be compared or combined. This method is useful because people tend to treat their first interpretation as an anchor and may overlook evidence that challenges it. Deliberately producing another perspective can expose assumptions that the initial judgment left unexamined. Nevertheless, two answers from the same person are not equivalent to two fully independent people: both may share the same background knowledge, biases, and blind spots. Internal reconsideration is therefore a useful supplement when time or access to others is limited, but it should not be mistaken for a complete substitute for outside feedback. The method is most useful when the second attempt genuinely explores a different line of reasoning rather than simply repeating the first answer with minor adjustments.
Study instruction:
- Write down an initial judgment, then generate an alternative judgment using different assumptions and explain which parts of the two perspectives remain correlated.
Key point:
- Deliberate reconsideration can expose errors in an initial judgment.
- Internal diversity is useful, but independent external perspectives can provide additional protection against shared blind spots.
Source links:
- The Wisdom of Many in One Mind: Improving Individual Judgments With Dialectical Bootstrapping
28. Q: How can making two estimates improve judgment, and why might a time delay help?
Show AnswerA: People can improve an estimate by making a second judgment that draws on a different interpretation of the available information. If both judgments contain different errors, comparing or averaging them can reduce the effect of an unusually high or low first estimate. A time delay may help because it allows the original response to become less immediately accessible, making the second attempt less dependent on the first. Research by Stefan Herzog and Ralph Hertwig describes this broader approach as dialectical bootstrapping, a method for using alternative judgments to improve individual estimates. The benefit depends on the second answer being meaningfully different and the question being suitable for estimation. If both attempts rely on exactly the same assumptions, averaging them may accomplish little. Averaging is also not appropriate when a question has a single factual answer that can be verified directly. The method should be treated as a way to improve uncertain judgments, especially where immediate independent information is unavailable, rather than as a replacement for evidence or measurement.
Study instruction:
- Make two independent estimates for an uncertain quantity, record the assumptions behind each, and compare their average with the best available reference value.
Key point:
- Independent estimates can reduce the influence of individual estimation errors.
- A second estimate is most useful when it introduces a genuinely different perspective.
Source links:
- The Wisdom of Many in One Mind — Psychological Science
29. Q: What is dialectical bootstrapping, and how can it be applied to a difficult decision?
Show AnswerA: Dialectical bootstrapping is a judgment-improvement technique in which a person deliberately generates a second estimate using a different perspective and combines the results. Instead of treating the first answer as definitive, the person assumes it may be inaccurate and actively searches for reasons. A practical sequence is to record the first estimate, question its assumptions, identify evidence that could make it too high or too low, formulate a second estimate, and compare the two. Averaging can be useful when both estimates are plausible and their errors are not perfectly correlated. For decisions that are not numerical, the same principle can be adapted by generating an alternative explanation or listing reasons the initial interpretation might be wrong. The method does not guarantee accuracy, especially if both perspectives rely on the same incomplete information. It is best used to expose uncertainty, broaden the reasoning process, and identify what additional evidence would be most valuable before an important decision is finalized.
Study instruction:
- Apply the five-step process to a budget forecast or project timeline and document how the alternative assumptions change the estimate.
Key point:
- Dialectical bootstrapping deliberately creates an alternative judgment to challenge the first.
- Comparing estimates should reveal assumptions and uncertainty, not merely generate an average.
Source links:
- The Wisdom of Many in One Mind: Improving Individual Judgments With Dialectical Bootstrapping
30. Q: How does the instruction to consider the opposite help people challenge an initial belief?
Show AnswerA: Considering the opposite means deliberately investigating how an initial judgment could be incorrect. Rather than searching only for evidence that confirms the first interpretation, the person identifies the strongest plausible alternative and asks what would need to be true for that alternative to be correct. For example, a manager who believes a project failed because an employee performed poorly could also examine whether the project had an unrealistic schedule, unclear requirements, or inadequate resources. This does not mean automatically rejecting the first judgment. It creates a disciplined opportunity to test whether the original explanation survives scrutiny. The technique is useful because people often generate reasons in support of their existing views more readily than they generate reasons against them. A good process requires specific counterarguments, relevant evidence, and a comparison of competing explanations. Where the decision is important, another person can review the alternatives independently. The objective is not indecision, but a more balanced evaluation before committing to a conclusion.
Study instruction:
- Choose a strongly held work-related belief and write the most credible alternative explanation, the evidence that would support it, and the evidence that would distinguish the two explanations.
Key point:
- Challenging an initial belief reduces reliance on confirmation alone.
- A credible alternative must be evaluated against evidence, not accepted merely because it differs.
Source links:
- The Wisdom of Many in One Mind — Psychological Science
31. Q: What can the virtual-reality self-conversation experiment teach us about perspective-taking?
Show AnswerA: The chapter describes an experiment in which participants discussed personal difficulties while switching between an avatar resembling themselves and an avatar representing a therapist. This arrangement created a structured opportunity to consider the same problem from two apparent perspectives. Participants who switched between the roles reported more beneficial changes than those who spoke only with the therapist avatar. The research suggests that changing the perspective from which a problem is considered can make reflection more useful for some people. It does not establish that a particular virtual-reality technique is necessary or that self-dialogue will resolve every psychological difficulty. The transferable idea is to create some distance from one's immediate emotional interpretation and examine the situation from a different standpoint. A person might ask what advice they would give a colleague facing the same issue or what an independent reviewer would need to know before reaching a conclusion. This technique can support reflection, but significant or persistent mental-health concerns may require qualified professional assistance.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a personal or workplace difficulty as if it belonged to a colleague, then identify what changes in your interpretation and proposed response.
Key point:
- Perspective-taking can create useful distance from an emotionally loaded problem.
- Self-reflection is a practical tool, not a substitute for professional care when that care is needed.
Source links:
- On an Effective and Efficient Method for Exploiting the Wisdom of the Inner Crowd
32. Q: How can a person use an internal devil's advocate to investigate a sticking point?
Show AnswerA: An internal devil's advocate is a deliberately adopted perspective that questions the assumptions, conclusions, or emotional interpretations held by the person's initial viewpoint. One role identifies the problem and explains the current belief; the other asks what might be missing, what evidence is uncertain, and what alternative explanation deserves attention. For example, a person who believes that a failed presentation proves they lack professional ability could examine whether the presentation was poorly prepared, the audience had different needs, or the experience revealed a specific skill that needs improvement. The process is useful because it separates the initial emotional reaction from a more analytical review. It should remain constructive rather than becoming an exercise in self-criticism. The objective is to identify plausible alternative interpretations and practical next steps, not to dismiss genuine difficulties. Writing the two perspectives down can make assumptions easier to compare and can help reveal what additional feedback or evidence would be most useful.
Study instruction:
- Write a short dialogue between an initial viewpoint and a devil's advocate, ensuring that each challenge is specific and each conclusion is linked to possible evidence.
Key point:
- A structured internal disagreement can expose assumptions hidden in the first interpretation.
- Constructive questioning should lead to investigation or action rather than endless self-criticism.
Source links:
- The Wisdom of Many in One Mind — Psychological Science
33. Q: Why can a second creative idea be better than the first idea a person generates?
Show AnswerA: The first idea often reflects the most accessible associations, familiar categories, and established assumptions. Because it appears quickly, people may interpret that speed as evidence that the idea is the best one. A second attempt can require the person to move beyond the first obvious possibility and explore less familiar alternatives. The chapter describes research on generating fitness-equipment ideas in which independent consumers often preferred participants' second ideas to their first. This illustrates why initial preferences should not be confused with final quality. The finding does not mean that every second idea is superior or that later ideas should automatically be preferred. Rather, it supports generating multiple distinct options before selecting one. The method is especially useful when the first solution resembles familiar products or repeated approaches that have failed to solve the problem. After generating alternatives, evaluate their usefulness, feasibility, originality, risks, and fit with the intended objective. A comparison of genuinely different options provides a stronger basis for choice than commitment to the first idea alone.
Study instruction:
- Generate at least three alternatives to a familiar solution before evaluating which one best meets the stated requirements.
Key point:
- First ideas can reflect entrenched assumptions rather than the strongest available solution.
- Generating alternatives broadens the choice set before evaluation begins.
Source links:
- Team Creativity, Cognition, and Cognitive Style Diversity
34. Q: How can someone create a systematic process for getting unstuck?
Show AnswerA: A practical unsticking process begins with a precise description of the obstacle and the result that would count as progress. Next, record what has already been attempted, what those attempts revealed, and which assumptions remain untested. Determine whether the next useful input should come from an expert, an unfamiliar outsider, a diverse group, or a structured internal reconsideration. Generate multiple possible approaches without immediately committing to the first one. Select a low-cost, reversible experiment where possible and define the evidence that would justify continuing, revising, or abandoning it. Review the results and update the problem definition if the original explanation no longer fits. If the experiment fails, record what was learned rather than simply repeating the same method. For high-risk problems, include appropriate professional review before acting. This process turns being stuck from a vague feeling into a sequence of testable decisions. It also helps prevent unproductive repetition by making previous attempts and the evidence they generated explicit.
Study instruction:
- Apply the sequence to a real problem and document the obstacle, previous attempts, missing perspectives, proposed experiment, expected evidence, and next decision.
Key point:
- Systematic problem-solving replaces repeated instinctive attempts with evidence-based experimentation.
- Every attempt should produce either progress or information that improves the next attempt.
Source links:
- How Does Diversity Affect Team Cognitive Processes? — Academy of Management Annals
35. Q: How can a team determine which outsiders are most likely to provide useful perspectives?
Show AnswerA: Begin with the specific knowledge gap rather than recruiting outsiders simply because they are different. If a team is struggling with usability, it may benefit from intended users, accessibility specialists, or product designers. If the problem concerns a complex data process, statisticians, software engineers, and operational staff may reveal different constraints. The most useful contributors are those who bring information or ways of thinking that are missing from the current discussion. Consider both the person's relevant expertise and the distinctiveness of their perspective. Also assess whether the contributor can understand the problem sufficiently to offer informed suggestions. Not every outsider needs to be an expert in the entire problem, but the information they provide must be appropriate to the question they are addressing. A targeted invitation should explain the problem, the constraints, and what kind of contribution is needed. Afterward, evaluate what new information the contributor introduced and whether it meaningfully changed the team's options or assumptions.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a contributor-selection matrix that links each identified knowledge gap to a potential source of relevant expertise or a different perspective.
Key point:
- The best outsider is selected for the perspective or knowledge the problem lacks.
- Diversity should serve a defined purpose rather than be treated as a decorative team characteristic.
Source links:
- How Does Diversity Affect Team Cognitive Processes? — Academy of Management Annals
36. Q: How should contributions from a crowd be moderated, prioritized, and validated?
Show AnswerA: A useful crowdsourcing process separates idea generation from final acceptance. During generation, contributors should be able to propose different approaches without having every unconventional suggestion rejected immediately. During evaluation, reviewers organize submissions, identify duplicates, examine the reasoning and available evidence, and compare the candidates against agreed requirements. Moderation can improve clarity, maintain relevance, and remove abusive or inappropriate submissions. Prioritization criteria should reflect the purpose of the exercise, such as originality, feasibility, safety, cost, expected benefit, or ease of testing. Promising proposals should then undergo appropriate validation before implementation. This may involve user testing, technical review, independent calculation, controlled trials, or professional assessment. In high-stakes areas, qualified decision-makers must retain responsibility for approval. Crowdsourcing platforms can also introduce bias if a small group dominates discussion, incentives favor popularity, or contributors imitate early answers. A transparent evaluation process helps preserve meaningful differences while preventing volume, confidence, or popularity from being mistaken for quality.
Study instruction:
- Build an evaluation rubric for a mock crowd challenge and test it on several contributions with different levels of originality and evidential support.
Key point:
- Generating possibilities and validating solutions are separate activities.
- Moderation and clear criteria help keep a crowd's output useful and reliable.
Source links:
- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
37. Q: How can leaders prevent groupthink while maintaining coordination and accountability?
Show AnswerA: Groupthink becomes a risk when the desire for agreement discourages people from expressing concerns or examining alternatives. Leaders can reduce this risk by inviting independent judgments before group discussion, assigning someone to challenge the leading proposal, seeking feedback from outside the immediate team, and requiring evidence for important conclusions. It is also helpful to distinguish the right to contribute from the responsibility to substantiate a proposal. Participants should be able to disagree without facing personal retaliation, while decisions still need clear ownership and deadlines. After alternatives have been considered, the team should document its selected option, the reasons for rejecting significant alternatives, and any unresolved risks. This approach preserves coordination without requiring artificial unanimity. Leaders should also examine their own behavior: announcing a strong preference too early may discourage people from offering contrary views. A constructive decision process makes disagreement useful, ensures that relevant perspectives are considered, and allows the group to act once the evidence and trade-offs have been sufficiently examined.
Study instruction:
- Review a recent team decision and identify whether dissenting views were invited, documented, and evaluated before the final decision was made.
Key point:
- Healthy disagreement can reveal assumptions that agreement alone would leave untested.
- Clear decision ownership allows teams to explore alternatives without becoming permanently indecisive.
Source links:
- How Does Diversity Affect Team Cognitive Processes? — Academy of Management Annals
38. Q: How can organizations measure whether diversity and crowdsourcing actually improve innovation?
Show AnswerA: Measurement should begin with a clear definition of success. Depending on the task, relevant outcomes may include solution quality, originality, implementation time, user satisfaction, error reduction, cost, or measurable improvements in performance. Organizations should distinguish inputs, processes, and outcomes. The number of contributors is an input, the range of ideas evaluated is a process measure, and the quality of the implemented solution is an outcome measure. A successful crowdsourcing effort may produce few accepted ideas but still identify a critical overlooked risk; conversely, thousands of submissions may create little value if none improves the decision. Where possible, compare results with a baseline or a similar task completed without the intervention. Account for changes in scope, resources, and external conditions before attributing improvement to diversity. Organizations should also assess whether new perspectives influenced decisions or were merely collected. A measurement framework should combine quantitative indicators with documented evidence of learning, assumptions challenged, and changes implemented.
Study instruction:
- Develop three outcome measures and two process measures for an innovation initiative, defining the data source, baseline, review period, and decision each measure will inform.
Key point:
- Participation and idea volume do not by themselves prove that innovation improved.
- Measure the quality and impact of results against clearly defined objectives.
Source links:
- Gender-diverse teams produce more novel and higher-impact scientific ideas — PNAS
39. Q: What common mistakes prevent people from benefiting from diverse perspectives?
Show AnswerA: Several recurring mistakes undermine the value of diverse input. Teams may recruit people with different backgrounds but ignore their contributions, invite outsiders too late for their advice to affect the outcome, or dismiss unfamiliar proposals because they conflict with established practice. Others may mistake novelty for quality and adopt unconventional ideas without sufficient testing. Crowdsourcing can fail when the problem is poorly defined, contributors lack essential context, incentives reward popularity rather than accuracy, or no one is responsible for evaluating submissions. Internal reflection can also become unproductive when the second perspective merely repeats the first or turns into excessive self-criticism. These problems can be reduced by defining the problem, identifying the missing perspective, providing appropriate context, documenting alternative explanations, and establishing criteria for evaluation. A successful intervention must lead to a decision, experiment, or useful clarification. If different perspectives are collected but never examined seriously, the organization has increased activity without necessarily improving its problem-solving ability.
Study instruction:
- Audit a past brainstorming or consultation exercise and identify whether it failed at problem definition, contributor selection, participation, evaluation, or implementation.
Key point:
- Different perspectives create potential value but do not guarantee useful decisions.
- A defined problem and a credible evaluation process prevent novelty from becoming noise.
Source links:
- How Does Diversity Affect Team Cognitive Processes? — Academy of Management Annals
40. Q: What ethical and practical limitations should be considered when using crowdsourcing?
Show AnswerA: Crowdsourcing may expose sensitive information, create unrealistic expectations, reward unqualified advice, or allow a popular but incorrect interpretation to spread. Participants may also contribute substantial effort without receiving appropriate recognition or compensation. Before launching a challenge, organizations should establish what information can be shared, obtain any necessary consent, protect personal and confidential data, and specify how contributions will be used. They should also assess intellectual-property issues, incentives, potential conflicts of interest, and whether participation could create harm for vulnerable individuals. In medicine and other high-stakes fields, safeguards must ensure that crowd suggestions do not substitute for professional judgment or required oversight. Practical limitations include the time spent screening low-quality responses, difficulties maintaining confidentiality, and the possibility that the most valuable contributors will not participate. These issues do not eliminate the benefits of crowdsourcing, but they require proportionate governance. A responsible process balances access to new perspectives with informed consent, appropriate validation, fair treatment of contributors, and accountability for decisions.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a risk checklist covering confidentiality, consent, contributor incentives, intellectual property, quality review, and responsibility for implementation.
Key point:
- Crowdsourcing requires safeguards proportionate to the sensitivity and risk of the problem.
- Organizations remain responsible for decisions made using external contributions.
Source links:
- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
41. Q: How can a team test whether introducing new perspectives helps solve a recurring problem?
Show AnswerA: Run a bounded experiment with a clearly defined starting point and success criterion. First, describe the recurring problem and document what the team has already tried. Record the current approach and its results so that subsequent changes can be evaluated against a baseline. Then introduce a carefully selected new perspective, such as an external reviewer, a colleague from another department, or a structured alternative-analysis exercise. Ask the contributor to identify assumptions, generate alternatives, and explain the reasoning behind the strongest suggestions. Choose one or two proposals for a practical test rather than changing the entire process at once. Monitor the relevant outcome, the time required, and any unintended consequences. At the end of the experiment, decide whether to adopt, adapt, or reject the intervention. The team should also record what was learned about the problem itself. A small, well-documented experiment gives the organization more reliable feedback than a broad initiative whose effects are difficult to separate from other changes.
Study instruction:
- Design a two-week experiment involving one external perspective, one measurable outcome, a baseline, and a documented decision at the end.
Key point:
- Small experiments help test whether new perspectives create practical value.
- Record the outcome and learning, including when the intervention fails to improve performance.
Source links:
- Networks, Creativity, and Time: Staying Creative through Brokerage and Network Rejuvenation
42. Q: How can the principles of diversity and crowdsourcing be applied to long-term personal development?
Show AnswerA: Personal development can become repetitive when people rely exclusively on their existing strengths, familiar advisers, and established interpretations of success. Growth requires opportunities to encounter different expectations, methods, and explanations. A person can broaden these opportunities by seeking feedback from people outside their immediate professional circle, collaborating across functions, studying unfamiliar disciplines, and deliberately reconsidering assumptions after unsuccessful attempts. It is equally important to test advice rather than accepting it based on the status of the person who provides it. A practical development plan should identify the capability to improve, the perspective currently missing, the people or experiences that could provide it, and the experiment that will demonstrate whether learning has occurred. Periodic reflection helps determine whether a new approach is producing results or merely creating activity. Personal development is not about abandoning one's identity or constantly chasing novelty. It is about expanding the range of responses available when established methods no longer serve the desired objective.
Study instruction:
- Create a personal learning plan with one recurring obstacle, one missing perspective, one new source of feedback, and one observable outcome to review after a defined period.
Key point:
- Personal growth benefits from experiences and feedback that challenge familiar assumptions.
- Deliberate experimentation turns new perspectives into practical learning.

- The Wisdom of Many in One Mind: Improving Individual Judgments With Dialectical Bootstrapping
